The Austrian population has indeed done violent things in these months. It is unique in Austria in post-war history, but also in Europe that we reduce the budget deficit by 4% of gross domestic product in just one and a half years, by 100 billion schilling. This is an enormous achievement that the Austrians accomplish. We want that no longer to be necessary in the future. Therefore, we have presented a very careful budget programme based on structural reforms, which will reduce the federal budget deficit to 2.1 percent. In accordance with the legal provisions on pages 16 and the following of this budget programme, ladies and gentlemen, the specific measures are presented in detail. As an example, I should like to mention the staffing effort. There is a list of nearly 15 points which show in detail the effects of staffing and final budgetary planning at the end of this report. For example, zero-on rounds with one-off payments in 1996, 1997; or reduction of overtime, additional fees; Restrictions of Pragmatization; new right of service for new entrants; more management positions on time and more. This is a variety of measures already prepared by federal legislation through the Structural Adjustment Act, but a variety of measures are also concrete projects. This time, we have undertaken to make reforms and structural measures very consistent in each policy field according to the best practice model – comparisons with other countries, as is made effective in the individual policy field. We also have the time to do so – thank God – since we already have a budget for two years. In part, the individual measures mentioned as projects still need to be drawn up – that is clear, because this is a budget programme by the year 2000. But all the others who have already decided are presented in detail, and the economic consequences of this are also included in the federal estimates in 1996 and 1997.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e2454	Additional question, how do I accept? - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e2469	Mr Finanzminister! The budget programme also mentions something about extra-budgetary financing, about the introduction of the comprehensive toll. As you know, our out-of-budget financing, i.e. debt that is not included in the budget, is also a major problem. ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e2487	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e2503	Ladies and gentlemen! This gives me an opportunity to address a very essential point: Will Austria reach the stability criteria that allow us to participate in monetary union? – As you know, there are five of these criteria. The first is the inflation criterion. This will be achieved through the very low inflation rate in our country. The second is the exchange rate criterion. This will be achieved in Austria due to its stable schilling, which has low fluctuations. The third is the deficit criterion. As you know, we will surely also achieve this, with a maximum deficit of 3 percent of the gross domestic product, countries and municipalities, through the measures taken by the Austrian population. The fourth criterion is long-term interest rates. This criterion will achieve Austria due to the good stability of its currency and credible fiscal policy. There is a problem: the fifth criterion: the so-called debt ratio, which should not exceed 60 percent of the gross domestic product, or the trend towards reducing the debt ratio to this 60 percent must be sustainable enough. On the basis of our plans, the budget programme shows that, without specific measures, we will still have to expect to build up the debt ratio to about 70 percent by 1998. Therefore, the Federal Ministry of Finance has launched a programme in good agreement with the countries and communities through discretionary measures, i.e. exceptional measures, in order to reduce this debt ratio as early as 1997, i.e. the turnaround towards 60 percent – which is sufficient for the assessment of this stability criterion – already in 1997. Three measures are essential to this end: The first measure is privatisation. We believe that we can reach about 1 per cent of gross domestic product – about 25 billion schilling – in debt reduction through privatisation proceeds from ATW, ATS, CA, Bank Austria, PSK – at least, I would say carefully. Secondly, We have tied up a package called "Change of the federal loans", in German the sales of claims – an approach that is more than usual in companies. Currently, demand sales of the Environmental Water Economic Fund are taking place against farms – gross in size, that is, nominal, about 6 billion schilling, cash value about 4.5 billion schilling. Demand sales from the Environmental Water Economic Fund to municipalities are still possible. This is a total of about 70 billion Schilling Nominale. It is estimated that a call for tender will allow for a cash value of between 40 and 45 billion Schilling debt reduction. The third set of measures is what you have mentioned. In fact, it is a question of allowing the definition of this criterion of stability not to have to include in the debt sum the debts of those budgets which cover more than 50 percent, as you have rightly pointed out, of private revenue. We have already started a very detailed work – here a great deal of thanks to the employees of the Municipal Government and so on – as we advise the municipalities and the cities in order to be able to take such tax budgets out of the debt calculation according to the definitions. These include, for example, water supply or waste management, wastewater associations, which are usually financed by more than 50 percent from private revenue. This is one of the points you have mentioned that it will be possible to avoid the current, quite large debt of between 70 and 80 billion schilling, which had to be spent to build the high-performance road network, i.e. the motorways and these things more, when 50 percent of private revenue comes. These include the toll revenue, the vignette, for example, but also the road use charge to be paid by the trucks today, and so on. This means that, without increasing the tolls already known and planned for the high-performance road network, we will be able to avoid having to include these existing debts in the Maastricht definition. Austria will already succeed in 1997 – this is not a huge, dramatic savings programme, as one of the Members said yesterday. . these measures will enable us to significantly reduce the debt ratio by simply accounting, so that we already have a 60 percent turnaround in 1997 and thus the fifth stability criterion for achieving monetary union.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e5680	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! High house! In today's agricultural debate, we are discussing, among other things, the Green Report and the distribution effect of agricultural transfers. I would like to give my first opinion on these two points. The Green Report shows an average income increase of 22 percent for 1995. However, I do not think it permissible to compare agriculture one year with the other, because – this is known – the weather has a great impact on the yield situation in agriculture. However, when analyzing income increases within four years, it must be noted that agricultural income has grown by around 7.5 percent since 1991. This does not even correspond to the rate of inflation granted to farmers, so that this result actually represents a loss of real wages. On the other hand, the income gap between farmers and other occupations is still enormous. If the agricultural income is converted to a monthly income, it must be noted that miners earn an average of 12 000 s per month, while valley farmers have a little more, but at least only 16 000 s of monthly income. This means a huge distance compared to other professional groups, so that I can only support the statements made in the Committee that double-digit income increases in the next period are important for the conservation of rural areas and agriculture. It is also my concern to present the Green Report as a whole for discussion. It is unquestionably true that only 2% of all farms are selected to represent a level of income of agriculture. As the Green Report in the text itself says, not all farmers are even taken into account and represented, so that this form of income representation in agriculture cannot be used as a representation of the real situation. In addition, transitional aid will be provided for a period of four years, the amounts of which will already be constantly reduced and which will even expire in four years. If one did not take these degressive compensation payments into account, there would be no 22 percent increase in income but a 2% reduction. In addition, payments for storage depreciation at maize, which should have actually become income-effective in 1994, have also been paid out in 1995, which clearly makes the picture of the year 1995 worse. There is therefore no reason at all for us liberals to break out here in the cry of jubilation, as your party observer Dr. Schüssel did in public. He must have done a bear service to the farmers! They share the responsibility that the English are now collapsing the beef market. That's a fifth of the actual damage. I am also surprised that Minister Molterer supports the policy of the EU, which, through intervention purchases, takes short-term beef from the market, but does not solve the problem because it is only temporarily shifted. That was also an essential reason why we are critical of EU accession and are still critical. In our view, there must be a change in the form of transfer payments, not only in Austria, but in Europe. However, the national scope which the European Union gives us is not enough to secure the employment situation in agriculture. There is nothing to be done, Mr Schwarzenberger, if your group is now trying to bring about improvements with the amounts of the base that are related to the operation. Of course, the first approaches are to correct this misunderstanding, but in fact there is far too little room to carry it out so efficiently that it is more than white cosmetics. We therefore maintain our call for a renationalisation of income policy – not a renationalisation of the whole agricultural policy, but a renationalisation of income policy; a renationalisation that fishermen have already indicated in connection with the enlargement of the East. Only he meant it in a way that did not fit us! In fact, he meant: You can have the skills, but the money remains in Brussels. – That’s not how it can be done! It is crucial that the contribution is reduced in order to ultimately ensure an independent agricultural policy in the direction of agricultural income security with the existing financial scope. How high these fixed amounts are to be, is a question which must be discussed politically and which needs to be carefully balanced and calculated. However, it must be made clear to the public that transfer payments are now only really enforceable when it comes to securing jobs, ensuring jobs also in rural areas, and that there should be equal inclusion of co-employees and full-time farmers. It does not make sense, as Mr Wurmitzer has said, but of this we are used to, to fight with foam in front of the mouth, but it would be very good if you thought about what measures could improve the employment situation in rural areas. ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e6690	Mr President. High house! Allow me to take this Green Report, the first Green Report since Austria's membership in the European Union, to give rise to some fundamental findings. First: Given the experience of the past year 1995 and 1996, as well as international development, I would like to make it quite clear from the Federal Government and the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry that Austria's accession was also the right step from the point of view of agriculture, the necessary step to which there is no alternative, because it is also the better for the Austrian farmers in the long term. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Secondly, The Green Report clearly confirms that the 1994 Europe Agreement was necessary and correct for Austrian agriculture, which was important to farmers. And I and the Federal Government are insisting that this Europe agreement should, of course, be implemented for agriculture in the future. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] Thirdly, This accession has led to changes in the agricultural system of support and market rules. It has brought about changes that have been debated in this House for years and decades – for example, I recall the debates on the function of the market order, on the function of export refunds, export support, on the fact that many groups have asked whether a change to direct payments would be the better way for farmers. The accession to the European Union has brought exactly this change, so that the subsidies and compensatory payments now come directly to the farmers and the market system is only a framework at European level. Fourth: Accession to the EU and the first 16, 18 months of our membership show that we have new opportunities and opportunities for farmers, for rural areas which are also used, for example in the areas of structural policy, regional support, environmental policy and producer communities. Fifth, you open a bottle This Green Report shows that we cannot yet be satisfied with all the developments, for example with the question of the market position of Austrian products, with the question of the processing structure, the marketing structure, these are areas in which we are still in the middle of the transition and have not yet achieved the optimum – I say this very openly from my point of view. What are the consequences of accession, which are shown from the point of view of agriculture in this Green Report? – It is true that the adoption of the common agricultural policy has reduced prices for farmers, depending on the product. However, it is equally clear in this Green Report that the measures taken by the European Treaty in 1995 achieved a balance of income in principle, for example through the destocking of stocks, the degressive compensatory payments, the market reform premium in the Union, the environmental programme or the compensatory allowance in the less favoured regions. The Green Report also shows clearly that the opening up of markets for Austria does not have the consequences that one had feared before accession, namely that a large flood of the Austrian market would result in products from countries in competition with us. From this point of view, I am very grateful to consumers, because they have kept the high-quality products of Austrian farmers to a high degree in this first year or in the first and a half years of our membership, and I ask for this confidence in the future. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We have also taken advantage of the opportunities given to us in the export. As far as Italy is concerned, for example, Austria's export share increased by 83 percent in 1995. For the first time in the history of agriculture, we have a positive trade balance vis-à-vis Italy. [[Abg. Ing. Reichhold: What about Germany?]] In Germany, Mr Reichhold, the situation is slightly different because Italy is a demand market for raw agricultural products, such as cereals, meat and milk, while in Germany we are dealing with a sharp competitive market where we have to supply processing products. [[Abg. Ing. Reichhold: What did we earn in Italy? - Nothing!]] It is clear that in this area, at least as far as the processing structure and the export structure are concerned, we need additional scales. Accession to the EU has led to an ecological shift through our environmental programme. This programme is considered to be an example in Europe, and we note the widest participation of farms in this environmental programme of all the Member States of the European Union. In the first one and a half years, we started the regional development programmes positively, and new impetus has been given to these opportunities by the European Union in many regions of Austria. Ladies and gentlemen! For example, we have approved investment support for the processing industry for 142 projects in the sector plan. This corresponds to a funding volume of 980 million schilling from the federal, state and European Union funds. After all, this causes an investment volume of 4.1 billion schilling. We have taken the first major steps in the field of producer communities, which I am proud of. However, more needs to be done in this regard. The farmers themselves must also be actively involved in this market position. In my view, the 1995 income report shows that we have managed well the year 1995, the first year of our membership of the European Union. It shows that, for farmers, compensation is necessary, correct and justified. It shows that we have taken first steps in the area of cost relief, which must follow. It shows that we are making positive use of the Union's structural effects, for example for farms in mountain regions and other disadvantaged areas. It also shows, however, that as regards the absolute level of income of farmers, it has still not been achieved that of the rest of the population, and it also shows clearly that we must not make the mistake of the average for each region, for every farm and every sector of agriculture. I am also referring to the current situation in the cattle sector, which, I believe, and the problems we face in Europe and thus also in Austria. The Austrian farmers who did not have a single BSE case, which produced high-quality products, have the same income and market problems as colleagues across the border. It is therefore necessary to put in place additional measures. [[Abg. Ing. Reichhold: You don't put one!]] The BSE balance with 335 million schilling is a first step. I would draw attention to the need for intervention, Mr Reichhold. In Austria, we have so far accommodated 27 000 pieces per intervention, which would otherwise cause additional market pressure. [[Abg. Ing. Reichhold: That doesn't solve the problem!]] For example, from Austria, 10 700 tonnes of meat have been marketed in third countries through the Union's third country refunds, which is also a market relief. However, these measures are not sufficient. It is therefore necessary to put in place additional measures in the cattle sector, such as increasing intervention, new categories such as entrants, measures already decided in the European Union. A new premium model will be to be developed and a decision will be made in the Council in October 1996 – I hope. We have brought in new impulses that are very important for Austrian production and for Austrian cattle producers, such as a model of calf marketing – we need this not only for male animals but also for female animals. We have launched further initiatives, such as a review of the European Union's imports. The issue of labelling in October 1996 will also be put forward in concrete terms, and we will support all initiatives to market lighter animals, because this will also relax the situation on the market. I am aware that additional efforts are needed at European level in the area of the beef market, as the situation for farmers is very, very difficult. Austria is also strongly committed to the further development of the European Union's policy that the European Union's environmental programme not only needs to be financed higher but also becomes a central pillar of agricultural policy. We have set new impulses with the Mining Monument, for example by discussing the amount of the base. In the meantime, other states, for example France, are following our memorandum. We need impulses in the area of green land, which I see not only in the area of land premium, but in particular in the beef sector. We also need to secure production rights, such as milk quotas, for the future; there is no doubt about this from an Austrian point of view. Building on meat labelling, we call for a comprehensive strategy in the field of food labelling and for standards, such as animal welfare, to be tightened at European level, also in the field of hormone discussion; Austria has never left a doubt about the quality and nature of its products! Ladies and gentlemen! The renationalisation was addressed. In this respect, I would like to say quite clearly: I am not in favour of the renationalisation of agricultural policy. However, I am very much in favour of achieving greater national scope in the field of support, in order to respond better to regional needs, such as the base amount. In conclusion, I would like to make a few remarks on the very constructive discussion on the future of agriculture so far: I agree with many that agriculture and agricultural policy must continue to be aimed at ensuring the highest possible share of income over the market, of production and of new market opportunities and of new production opportunities for farmers. This, however, requires, of course, restructurings which are already in progress, for example in the area of the processing industry, which requires additional efforts to be made together with the economy – in this respect I appeal – also in the area of marketing. This requires the farmers' impulses – the words of producer communities – and, of course, the rules of play that we have to discuss in this House on the occasion of the debate on the rules of commerce. That's not a question for me at all. However, it also requires a clear commitment to the compensatory payments, which are not a gift for farmers, but a contribution to the services provided by farmers. I also say quite clearly – I have already said this in committee – what my position on the question, which of course is very up-to-date – is about the discussion on the development of agricultural policy both in Austria and in the European Union. Ladies and gentlemen! I have always said that I want a clear and objective debate, whose central question is: What is the meaning of what support measures? Degressive compensatory payments have a different objective than the environmental programme. The compensation allowance in the mountain areas, for example, has a different objective than the market organisation premiums in the European Union. Ladies and gentlemen! On the basis of this motion for a resolution, I also believe it is necessary to state clearly that, in my view, there are four criteria for the agricultural policy debate: Criterion 1: economic situation, income situation of farmers also in international competition. Criterion 2: Environmental orientation of agricultural production and agricultural framework conditions. Criterion 3: Regional perspective also in the area of agricultural policy, because regions have different requirements. Criteria 4: It is clear to me that the question of social acceptance must also play a role in this debate. What I don't think is right is an emotional discussion, as we have already led them – everyone knows – to the more often. I am in favour of an objective information system. I shall therefore also, as this motion for a resolution calls for, provide the appropriate proposals in a timely manner to the House. Ladies and gentlemen! I would ask you to continue to support agricultural agriculture in the future. The farmers – we know – are not in a simple situation. It is also necessary to make further efforts to make it clear to the public that Austria needs these rural family farms, that Austria in Europe insists on this eco-social orientation of agricultural policy, that Austria in the European Union will continue to provide impetus for further development in the future. At this point, I should like to thank the farmers who have had to cope with major changes, the administration that has managed these changes well, and also the consumers - I repeat clearly - whose loyalty to Austrian products we need, because Austrian-agricultural products are of the quality in which the consumer can be sure that he has the best. - Thank you.

However, the Foundation model also leaves many, many questions open and will strengthen financial pressure, especially since it is intended to provide the federal government with concrete financial grants every year; the speech is about 200 million schilling. This will lead to a further increasing economic pressure on the Federal Forest towards rationalization, thus further shifting the basic task of forest owners, namely the conservation of forests, to the long bank. Perhaps this will allow a legislative period to survive, but in the long term it will be possible to ignore time limits. If one really ignores the conservation of forests, then there is a development in progress that can hardly be remedied so quickly – if only with the use of incredibly high financial resources. We therefore propose a completely different direction in this context; we have already tabled a motion for a resolution in mid-September. We believe that budgetary levies and profit-making targets must end where long-term habitat protection, quality of life, environmental conditions, is a key issue of the habitat in Austria. There must be a clear guarantee, and there must be an end to budgetary expenditure at all budgetary pressures. This would actually be a necessary and correct direction of impact, which should be supported by all those who are in favour of ecologistization, by all those who are representatives of forest owners. What we want, what we demand and what we ask for here with this request, is, first and foremost, that there is an order to the Federal Forest in the direction of ecologically sustainable management of the Federal Forest, that there is a clear reform of the ÖBF law in this context, that there is an exclusion from the Federal budget only in the face of the abolition of camera lysics and the introduction of a net budget that – a very essential point! – at least 50 percent of the balance sheet profit from the federal company ÖBF is to be invested in ecologically relevant measures, such as conservation forest renovation – that would be a very crucial and essential requirement – that the Economic Council be upgraded, while the Board of Directors can be reduced very much – I believe there is a savings potential; this has also clearly shown the staff survey within the Federal Forest that there is great dissatisfaction on the second, third, fourth level of the employees, namely the desire to save on the upper floors – and that the Board of the Federal Forests – also a very essential point in this request – to maintain or improve the qualitative standard in the field of forest care. This means reducing staff pressure, providing sufficient care staff in the patient's area of forest, especially in the protection forest area. I would be pleased if the Minister of Agriculture would give us an overview in this debate of the current state of this political goal, because I believe that the general public has a right to know how this ecologically crucial area – protection forest renovation, the future of the Austrian state forest – continues overall. Thank you.

Stadler: Your own minister said that!]] First: Since October 1995 – and thus for the first time for seven years – the inflation rate has been below 2%. This is mainly due to the lower food prices and prices for other goods of daily consumption, which in 1995 were more than 11 percent lower than before accession to the EU. Estimates of price damping effects show that the inflation rate would have been higher or higher in case of non-accession. Secondly, In 1995 alone, exports to EU countries increased by 11 per cent, while imports from EU countries were only 9 per cent. I refer to Switzerland as an example, because Switzerland as a non-EU member country is always cited as a slaring country. Austria's export increases have been significantly higher since its accession in 1995 than in Switzerland. Industrial and investment investments in Austria are significantly higher than in Switzerland. Imports to Austria and Switzerland have increased to about the same extent. This means, ladies and gentlemen, that the Austrian economy was well aware of the good conditions to use the improved conditions after accession to the EU. ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e11831	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen! There is no doubt that the Austrian Central Statistical Office had problems in producing data and statistics. These problems have been discussed in recent weeks and months by the staff of the Statistical Central Office in close cooperation with the Federal Chancellery, with institutes outside the Federal Chancellery, with the FGG and others, and with proposals for solutions. For all the statistics that were relevant to us, it has succeeded in finding and implementing solutions within a very short time. That means, ladies and gentlemen, The necessary external trade statistics for 1995 were completed in September 1996. The problems we had with other statistics, whether consumer index, unemployment statistics or other statistics, were all solved in the summer of 1996. I admit that the statistics were very necessary and important. In a very correct, very fast, very cooperative and partnership cooperation between all concerned, the problems have been solved. My goal is – and that is not only my goal, but also the goal of the staff of the Central Statistical Office – to further develop the Central Statistical Office in the future. There are a number of proposals, concepts and initiatives to this end. We are in the process of implementing this. One of the objectives is to shape the Statistical Central Office in the future in such a way that it is an institution that works in a very modern way in the private economy and in the future also receives revenue for the data it creates. My aim is to achieve a partial right of the Statistical Central Office. My aim is to use external data, which is currently being collected twice or triple, for the Statistical Central Office. Finally, my goal is to put together many areas in the public service – for example, the Federal Audit Office's computer service and the Central Statistical Office's computer. So, in summary, I can say: The criticism presented at the alleged data chaos in the Statistical Central Office is a criticism which may have been in part true in the first half of 1995, but this point has now been largely resolved satisfactoryly.

Doris Kammerlanderand comrades concerning a federal law amending the Party Law, the National Council's Rules of Procedure, the Club Finance Act 1985, the Federal Law on the Promotion of Political Education and Journalism 1984 and the National Council Election Regulation 1992 (promoting the participation of women in political life)
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e15630	We're now on the 10th. Agenda item: First reading of Parliament's motion for a resolution 146/A. Doris Kammerlander and comrades concerning a federal law that amends the party law and other law. We are going into the debate, and I begin by assigning Mrs Magistrate. Mr Kammerlander as an applicant for the floor.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-03-020-XX-NRSITZ-00041_d7e15657	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen. We have already tabled this request during the last legislative period and we have already done a first reading in Parliament at that time. It was then no longer possible to discuss the matter in committee by the early elections. I would therefore like to take a little shorter than a year ago, since we already presented the proposal in a very comprehensive way. But I would like to remind you once again of the essential points of our request which we have tabled. In principle, the aim of the proposal is to create the increase in the proportion of women through conscious financial incentives. We assume, I think, that the proportion of women in the Austrian Parliament, as well as in other parliamentary institutions, from the country days to the local authorities, is embarrassingly low; it is embarrassing to note that in 1918 the general, direct, secret right of election was introduced, which allowed women to vote and vote for the first time. At this first election in 1919, the proportion of women reached 5.5 percent. In this House, 70 years later, there was a special meeting on the subject of women's representation in parliaments, in democratic institutions. On this occasion, the then President of the National Council, Dr. Hubinek, said that the fair situation would only be achieved in 500 years if the current rate of change was maintained, namely 50 percent of the proportion of women. I think we all agree that we should not put up with this patience to wait 500 years, but that it is time to take concrete action. In our view, concrete measures are that an objective, a clear and binding objective should be defined in a given time frame. If we assume that a real quota, which also reflects the share of the population, is just over 50 percent, then Parliament should proceed in a time-consuming plan to define, as it were, in an action plan to be adopted, until what steps need to be taken to achieve this objective. In order to create an incentive to achieve this objective, to create an incentive for the individual candidate parties, we have considered linking it to the general promotion of parties, which currently totals more than 200 million schilling. It is not a question of increasing the volume of the volume, it is a question of linking a certain proportion, which accounts for about 15 percent of that volume, to whether the rate of women of 50 percent or the target of a party is being reached, or not, and thus to combine certain financial incentives. In other words, this promotion will be reduced by 15 percent for the first time. However, if a party reaches this target female ratio in relation to this 50 percent, which is generally to be achieved, the promotion of the parties, club financing and the reimbursement of the electoral costs will be increased accordingly. Of course, this cannot be all, nor is it all, but it should also be taken into account whether the individual parties also decide on comprehensive programmes for themselves, a set of measures to promote women in political life. I have once again looked at what my fellow Members from the other political groups said on our proposal at the time. Interestingly, there was no general rejection of these proposals in any political group at the time. We have agreed in all political groups that it is urgently necessary to raise this proportion of women in Parliament. Some Members of other political groups, such as the EIA, have also shown great interest in discussing such a model and in discussing in the Committee whether this can be implemented and whether we women expect it to work. In some parties at the time, I had a willingness to talk about it and to break my mind about whether this measure could be an appropriate measure among many others. I would like to reiterate this willingness today with our request to bring it closer to you once again, and to give you an interesting example at the international level, namely that of another national Parliament, as at the time. Sweden, like some other Nordic countries such as Norway, Finland, Denmark, is regarded as a model country for women's participation in politics. But that was not always the case, and this was not achieved in particular – I would particularly like to suggest this to my fellow Members – through appeals to reason, and it was not achieved by patient waiting and waiting, if perhaps sometime more women are represented in the ranks of the individual parties. And that seems to me to be essential. It was only achieved by adopting an action plan by Parliament in 1972, when the proportion of women was 14 per cent lower than that of us today, a worse starting point, which provided for a 30 per cent quota, after an evaluation with a 40 per cent quota, was extended once more until 1995 and has now been extended by 1998 after a recent evaluation with the aim of a 50 per cent quota. This action plan, with the clear, binding objective of achieving this objective, combined a bundle of measures that have affected active and effective support for women in political life. This ranged from training, support, transfer of responsibilities, family-friendly orientations of meetings to childcare facilities and financial support for such measures in the individual parties, in the individual institutions. This action plan, as we know, has now reached its goal in 1996, in 1995. I am sure that this action plan will reach its target of a 50 % quota in 1998. There are some countries that have followed this example. Norway has done this similarly, Finland has done this in a similar way, Denmark has done this in a similar way, and in recent years the Benelux States have also begun to pick up in this regard. Belgium and the Netherlands also have an action plan. That is to say, more and more countries are in line with what has also been stated at the level of the European Union in an action plan of the European Parliament. I think - this is the meaning of the request - that it is time for Austria to join in such activities and that we do not only allow it to appeal to reason, and that we do not just leave it in patience. There are also some examples among you. I remember that the major parties in particular have quotas. They also want to reach a certain quota, only they never reach it. They never reach them because the front seats, the man-made places are still filled by men. If you look at the statistics after the last election, it is noted that there has not been an essential improvement in the large parties, and in the small parties, except the Greens, there has even been a deterioration in the representation of women in Parliament. My request goes in the direction that you put up the patience to consider and discuss this once and to say a request that is there not to wipe from the table, not to say that it is too complicated, that it is unrealistic, but actually to discuss. I know that one year ago in the debate there was also the argument that women would be brought back, under quotation marks, "against the payment of the parties", that is, against a financial improvement. It is also not very nice word "cop money". . I must say that this does not bother me as long as the goal is that more women sit in Parliament. If we look, for example, at the Nordic countries, including the Benelux countries, we can see that women's programmes, that support programmes, that action plans also cost money. This is the meaning of this whole action: If these 15 percent are now intended for this achievement of the women's rate and therefore for the implementation and enforcement of women's promotion measures – in a bundle of measures that all have this objective – then this is a sensible and, above all, very targeting commitment, which is probably not easy to understand in some other areas and which is not so direct to the goal. I therefore think it is really time for Parliament to consider concrete measures, to discuss and decide on an action plan for Parliament, and also to set up appropriate measures for Parliament itself to promote more women in political life, to be in political decision-making positions. It is also time – and this is also a point that is dealt with in the request – to discuss this – to have a very sensitive political debate on secondary professions, secondary incomes and secondary professions, especially in the public sector behind us, that the work of a Member of Parliament is more and more a fuller activity because of the complex situation of information, of politics at all. It is an activity in which no regulation is provided for, for example, the situation of pregnancy, the birth of a child, for man and woman. We believe that it is time to discuss and find that a temporary voluntary relinquishing of a mandate at the time of birth should be possible, so to speak, a Quasicarence regime, for both of them, on a voluntary basis, and depending on how they wish to use it or not. The possibility of a return of the individual who wishes to take advantage of this should be created at any time. The emphasis is on voluntary, but this possibility should be granted in principle. Once again, my appeal is mainly to the women of the House to examine this request carefully and also to remember that we have something like this as an example. If it is not possible to achieve an blatantly higher proportion of women in this House in the foreseeable future, it will also be more difficult for women in the rural and municipal areas to have the necessary reputation and to be represented there with a quota of 50 percent. Remember: Everything we do here certainly has an effective and binding role model. Thank you.

What is true of the entire profession is also true of Parliament. Women constantly encounter the "glass ceiling" in the professional world, they suffer from double and triple stress. We should therefore also consider the extent to which the processes of our work are family hostile. I think it should be possible to work out a programme together, at least in some areas, so that we can solve the problems, not only of the mandates but also of the officials of the House. Ladies and gentlemen! I would now like to end my discussion by saying that we have to deal intensively and seriously with the proposals. There will also be other proposals and other possibilities. We should take time for this in the Committee on Equal Opportunities. I am convinced that it will be possible, with a little good will, to achieve something beyond tags to increase the proportion of women in the parties, in politics and in particular in Parliament.
